Index of Frequently asked questions Appendix C - Defined terms Separate and individual financial statements Overview IAS 27 Separate Financial Statements prescribes the accounting and disclosure requirements for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates when an entity prepares separate financial statements. Although IAS 27 does not mandate which entities produce separate financial statements it applies when an entity prepares separate financial statements that comply with IFRS accounting standards. This chapter deals not only with aspects of the preparation of separate financial statements but also with some of the consequences of common control or group transactions in individual or separate financial statements, and in a set of consolidated financial statements prepared for a sub-group as far as transactions with the ultimate parent or with other entities under common control, other than with respect to business combination under common control (see Chapter 11). What you need to know • Separate financial statements are those presented by an entity in which the entity could elect, subject to the requirements in IAS 27, to account for its investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates either at cost, in accordance with IFRS 9 Financial Instruments, or using the equity method as described in IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures. • The financial statements of an entity that does not have a subsidiary, associate or joint venture are not ‘separate financial statements’. • When an entity has no subsidiaries, but has investments in joint ventures or associates, there could be differences between separate financial statements and individual financial statements. • Guidance in IFRS accounting standards for the consequences of common control or group transactions in individual or separate financial statements is limited and incomplete. Whilst such intra-group transactions do not affect the consolidated financial statements of the parent, as they are eliminated during consolidation, they can have a significant impact on the separate or individual financial statements of the parent and/or subsidiaries and/or a set of consolidated financial statements prepared for a sub-group. Separate and individual financial statements Separate and individual financial statements Separate financial statements are defined as follows: [IAS 27.4]. Extract from IAS 27 The following terms are used in this Standard with the meanings specified: Consolidated financial statements are the financial statements of a group in which the assets, liabilities, equity, income, expenses and cash flows of the parent and its subsidiaries are presented as those of a single economic entity. Separate financial statements are those presented by an entity in which the entity could elect, subject to the requirements in this Standard, to account for its investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates either at cost, in accordance with IFRS 9 Financial Instruments, or using the equity method as described in IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures. Generally, the IASB takes the view that the needs of users of financial statements are fully met by requiring entities to consolidate subsidiaries and equity account for associates and joint ventures. However, it is recognised that entities with subsidiaries, associates or joint ventures may wish, or may be required by local law, to present financial statements in which their investments are accounted for on another basis, e.g., as equity investments or under the equity method. [IAS 27.2]. The IASB has collected information about the use of IFRS accounting standards around the world that also provides details on whether different jurisdictions have elected to require or permit entities to present financial statements in accordance with IAS 27.1 Accordingly, IFRS accounting standards do not require the preparation of separate financial statements. However, when an investor with subsidiaries, associates or joint ventures does prepare separate financial statements purporting to comply with IFRS accounting standards, they must be prepared in accordance with IAS 27. [IAS 27.3]. It follows from the definition of separate financial statements above, that the financial statements of an entity that does not have a subsidiary, associate or joint venture are not ‘separate financial statements’. [IAS 27.7]. This chapter also addresses matters that are not exclusive to separate financial statements but relate to any stand-alone financial statements prepared by any entity within a group. We have called these ‘individual financial statements’, although they may also be referred to (amongst other names) as ‘stand-alone’, ‘solus’ or ‘single-entity’ financial statements. The term ‘individual financial statements’ for the purpose of this chapter is a term that covers (i) financial statements of entities that do not have investments in associates, joint ventures and subsidiaries, and (ii) financial statements of entities that have only investments in associates and/or joint ventures. When an entity has no investments in associates, joint ventures and subsidiaries in both the current and comparative period, effectively there is no difference between individual and separate financial statements of such an entity. Figure 1-1 below summarises the interactions between consolidated, individual and separate financial statements. This is further discussed in section 1.1 below. Who uses IFRS Accounting Standards?, IFRS Foundation’s website (accessed 30 September 2023). Separate and individual financial statements Figure 1-1: Interactions between consolidated, individual and separate financial statements Notes: The entity prepares separate financial statements as its only financial statements. [IAS 27.8A]. In this situation IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements would require that investments in subsidiaries, associates and/or joint ventures are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9. Hence, the cost or equity method would not be available as an accounting policy choice to account for investments in subsidiaries, associates and/or joint ventures in the separate financial statements. If the entity is either not an investment entity, or it is an investment entity and has a subsidiary or subsidiaries that provide(s) investment- related services, the entity prepares consolidated financial statements. If the entity makes use of the exemption from preparing consolidated financial statements, the entity may prepare separate financial statements as its only IFRS financial statements. (See section 1.1 below). If the entity is not an investment entity, or it is an investment entity and has a subsidiary or subsidiaries that provide(s) investment-related services, and the entity has not made use of the exemption from preparing consolidated financial statements, the entity prepares consolidated financial statements. In addition, the entity may prepare separate financial statements. If the entity has an investment in an associate and/or a joint venture and the entity has made use of the exemption from applying the equity method to those investments, the entity may prepare separate financial statements as its only financial statements, in which investments in the associate/joint venture are accounted for at cost or in accordance with IFRS 9. The entity prepares individual financial statements and applies the equity method to its investment in an associate and/or a joint venture. Separate financial statements may be prepared in addition wherein the company may elect a different accounting policy to account for its investment in an associate or a joint venture, such as at cost or in accordance with IFRS 9. When an entity has no investments in subsidiaries, associates or joint ventures, the entity’s individual financial statements are identical to the separate financial statements. In certain jurisdictions these individual financial statements may be referred to as separate financial statements due to local Companies Act, or similar, naming conventions. However, as they do not have an investment in either a subsidiary, associate or joint venture, they do not meet the definition of separate financial statements in IAS 27. [IAS 27.7]. * The entity may, in certain circumstances (e.g., when the investment is held by, or indirectly through, an entity that is a venture capital organisation or mutual fund), elect to measure an investment in an associate or a joint venture at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9. [IAS 28.18]. If the entity elects this policy for all such investments in joint ventures and associates, the financial statements may be referred to as separate financial statements. If the election is not made for one or more investments the financial statements are individual financial statements. Transactions often take place between a parent entity and its subsidiaries or between subsidiaries within a group that may or may not be carried out at fair value. The objective of IAS 27 is only to prescribe the accounting and disclosure requirements for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates when an entity prepares separate financial statements. Therefore, there may be uncertainty and ambiguity about how these common control or group transactions should be accounted for. IAS 24 Related Party Disclosures requires only that these transactions are disclosed and provides no accounting requirements. Whilst such transactions do not influence the consolidated financial statements of the ultimate parent (as they are eliminated during consolidation), they can have a significant impact on the individual or separate financial statements of the entities concerned or on the consolidated financial statements prepared for a sub-group. These issues are discussed in section 4 below. Differences between the IFRS accounting standards and US GAAP guidance on this topic are not addressed in this chapter as the standards are not converged. Therefore, differences between IFRS accounting standards and US GAAP may exist that are not discussed in this chapter. Separate and individual financial statements 1.1 Consolidated financial statements and separate financial statements A parent is an entity that controls one or more entities. A parent entity, unless exempt, should present consolidated financial statements in which the assets, liabilities, equity, income, expenses and cash flows of the parent and its subsidiaries are presented as those of a single economic entity. [IFRS 10.4, IFRS 10 Appendix A]. Extract from IFRS 10 An entity that is a parent shall present consolidated financial statements. This IFRS applies to all entities, except as follows: (a) a parent need not present consolidated financial statements if it meets all the following conditions: (i) it is a wholly-owned subsidiary or is a partially-owned subsidiary of another entity and all its other owners, including those not otherwise entitled to vote, have been informed about, and do not object to, the parent not presenting consolidated financial statements; (ii) its debt or equity instruments are not traded in a public market (a domestic or foreign stock exchange or an over-the-counter market, including local and regional markets); (iii) it did not file, nor is it in the process of filing, its financial statements with a securities commission or other regulatory organisation for the purpose of issuing any class of instruments in a public market; and (iv) its ultimate or any intermediate parent produces financial statements that are available for public use and comply with IFRSs, in which subsidiaries are consolidated or are measured at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with this IFRS. This exemption is discussed further in Chapter 7 at section 2.2.1. It should be noted that if the entity chooses to prepare consolidated financial statements even if it is exempted from doing so it must comply with all IFRS accounting standards requirements related to consolidated financial statements. An entity that avails itself of the above exemption may prepare separate financial statements as its only financial statements. [IAS 27.8]. For example, most intermediate holding companies take advantage of this exemption. If such an entity prepares unconsolidated financial statements that are in accordance with IFRS accounting standards, they must comply with the provisions of IAS 27 for such statements and they will then be separate financial statements as defined. The requirements for separate financial statements are dealt with in section 2 below. Certain issues related to timing of issuance of such separate financial statements are also discussed in section 1.1.3. below. IFRS 10 includes an exception to the consolidation principle for a parent that meets the definition of an investment entity. An investment entity measures its investments in subsidiaries, other than those solely providing services that relate to its investment activities, at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 instead of consolidating those subsidiaries. The investment entity exception is discussed in # Chapter 7 at section 10. Investment entities measure their investments in those subsidiaries in the same way in their separate financial statements as required in the consolidated financial statements. [IAS 27.11A]. As a result, IAS 27 clarifies that an investment entity that is required, throughout the current period and all comparative periods presented, to apply the exception to consolidation for all its subsidiaries in accordance with IFRS 10.31 presents separate financial statements as its only financial statements. [IAS 27.8A, IFRS 10.31]. An investment entity that prepares separate financial statements as its only financial statements, discloses that fact and presents the disclosures relating to investment entities required by IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities about its interests in subsidiaries. [IAS 27.16A]. The exemption from preparing consolidated financial statements, in criterion (iv) above, is available to a parent entity that is a subsidiary of an investment entity, even when the investment entity does not prepare consolidated financial statements but measures its subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss. **[IFRS 10.BC28A, IFRS 10.BC28B].** The requirements to prepare consolidated and separate financial statements are very often subject to local jurisdictional rules regardless of whether these financial statements are prepared in accordance with local GAAP or IFRS accounting standards. However, IFRS 10 provides specific IFRS requirements which need to be considered when financial statements are prepared on the basis of IFRS accounting standards as issued by the IASB. [IFRS 10.20]. Separate and individual financial statements How we see it If a parent does not prepare IFRS consolidated financial statements due to a concession in local law, the parent could not present separate financial statements in compliance with IFRS accounting standards. This is also further discussed in Chapter 7 at section 2.2.5. See section 1.2 below regarding the interrelationship between IFRS accounting standards and local European law in respect of consolidated and separate financial statements. IFRS 10 requires a parent to consolidate a subsidiary until the date on which the parent ceases to control the subsidiary. [IFRS 10.20]. How we see it In our view, consolidated financial statements must be prepared by an entity that was a parent during the reporting period, even if that entity is no longer a parent at the end of the reporting period (e.g., because it disposed of all its subsidiaries). Entities should consider applicable local regulation requirements governing the preparation of IFRS Financial Statements. 1.1.1 Separate financial statements and interests in associates and joint ventures IAS 28 must be applied by ‘all entities that are investors with joint control of, or significant influence over, an investee’. [IAS 28.2]. IAS 28 requires that an investment in an associate or a joint venture be accounted for in the entity’s separate financial statements in accordance with IAS 27.10. [IAS 28.44, IAS 27.10]. An entity that is an investor may present separate financial statements as its only financial statements if it is a parent that is exempt from preparing consolidated financial statements by the scope exemption in IFRS 10.4(a) (see above). [IFRS 10.4(a)]. If it does not have subsidiaries it may still present separate financial statements as its only financial statements if the same criteria as in (i)-(iv) above apply (they are replicated in IAS 28.17) and all its other owners, including those not otherwise entitled to vote, have been informed about, and do not object to, the entity not applying the equity method to its investees. [IAS 28.17]. A parent cannot prepare financial statements in purported compliance with IFRS accounting standards in which subsidiaries are consolidated but associates and joint ventures are not accounted for under IAS 28 but on some other basis (e.g., at cost). Financial statements prepared on such a basis would be neither consolidated financial statements (because of the failure to apply IAS 28) nor separate financial statements (because of the failure to account for subsidiaries on the basis of the direct equity interest). The conditions for exemption in IAS 28.17 mentioned above are the same as those in IFRS 10. This means that (as illustrated in Figure 1-1 in section 1 above): • An entity that has subsidiaries and is exempt under IFRS 10 from preparing consolidated accounts is automatically exempt in respect of its associates or joint ventures as well, i.e., it does not account for them under IAS 28. • An entity that has associates or joint ventures, but no subsidiaries, and does not meet all the exemption criteria in section 1.1 above, is required to apply equity accounting to its associates in its own (non-consolidated) financial statements. Such non-consolidated financial statements include the investment in the associate or joint venture on the basis of the reported results and net assets of the investment. Unless the entity opts to account for all associates or joint ventures using the equity method in its separate financial statements (see section 2.3 below) such non-consolidated financial statements are not ‘separate financial statements’ as defined in IAS 27 (see definition above) and therefore do not have to meet the additional measurement and disclosure requirements required by IAS 27 for separate financial statements that are described in section 3 below to comply with IFRS accounting standards. If such an entity also presents separate financial statements, the Board expects that the entity is likely to account for its investments in associates or joint ventures either at cost or in accordance with IFRS 9. [IAS 27.BC10D]. Although IAS 27 also allows for a policy to use the equity method to account for investments in associates and joint ventures in the separate financial statements, this could be different to some national GAAPs, under which investors that have no subsidiaries (and therefore, do not prepare consolidated financial statements) but have associates or joint ventures, are not permitted to account for their share of the profits and net assets of associates or joint ventures in their statutory financial statements. Separate and individual financial statements How we see it We believe an entity that has held investments in associates and/or joint ventures during the reporting period is required to prepare individual financial statements at the end of period in which any such investments were sold or otherwise disposed of. This is the case even if they are the only investments of the investor and these investments were sold during the period. Entities should consider applicable local regulation requirements governing the preparation of IFRS Financial Statements. 1.1.2 Separate financial statements and interests in joint operations IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements differentiates between joint operations and joint ventures. In the separate financial statements, joint ventures are accounted for at cost, in accordance with IFRS 9, or using the equity method as required by IAS 27.10. A joint operator applies paragraphs 20 to 22 of IFRS 11 to account for a joint operation. [IFRS 11.26]. This means that regardless of the type of financial statements prepared, the joint operator in a joint operation recognises in relation to the joint operation its: • Assets, including its share of any assets held jointly • Liabilities, including its share of any liabilities incurred jointly • Revenue from the sale of its share of the output arising from the joint operation • Share of the revenue from the sale of the output by the joint operation And • Expenses, including its share of any expenses incurred jointly [IFRS 11.20] Similarly, in its individual financial statements, a party that participates in, but does not have joint control of, a joint operation, accounts for its interest as outlined above provided it has rights to the assets and obligations for the liabilities, relating to the joint operation (see Chapter 12 at section 6.4). [IFRS 11.23]. In March 2015, the IFRS IC published a number of agenda decisions relating to IFRS 11. The following two are relevant to separate financial statements:2 Frequently asked questions Question 1-1: How does a joint operator, in its separate financial statements, account for its share of the assets and liabilities of a joint operation when it is structured through a separate vehicle? The IFRS IC noted that IFRS 11 requires the joint operator to account for its rights and obligations in relation to the joint operation. It also noted that those rights and obligations, in respect of that interest, are the same regardless of whether separate or consolidated financial statements are prepared, by referring to IFRS 11.26. [IFRS 11.26]. Consequently, the same accounting is required in the consolidated financial statements and in the separate financial statements of the joint operator. The IFRS IC also noted that IFRS 11 requires the joint operator to account for its rights and obligations, which are its share of the assets held by the entity and its share of the liabilities incurred by it. Accordingly, the IFRS IC observed that the joint operator would not additionally (in its separate or consolidated financial statements) account for its shareholding in the separate vehicle, whether at cost or fair value. IFRIC Update, March 2015, p.11. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements Frequently asked questions Question 1-2: How does a joint operation that is a separate vehicle account for its share of assets and liabilities in its separate financial statements? This issue has arisen because the recognition by joint operators in both consolidated and separate financial statements of their share of assets and liabilities held by the joint operation leads to the question of whether those same assets and liabilities should also be recognised in the financial statements of the joint operation itself. The IFRS IC decided not to add the issue to its agenda, because sufficient guidance exists:3 (a) IFRS 11 applies only to the accounting by the joint operators and not to the accounting by a separate vehicle that is a joint operation. (b) The financial statements of the separate vehicle would therefore be prepared in accordance with applicable Standards. And (c) Company law often requires a legal entity/separate vehicle to prepare financial statements. Consequently, the reporting entity for the financial statements would include the assets, liabilities, revenues and expenses of that legal entity/separate vehicle. However, when identifying the assets and liabilities of the separate vehicle, it is necessary to understand the joint operators’ rights and obligations relating to those assets and liabilities and how those rights and obligations affect those assets and liabilities. 1.1.3 Issuing separate financial statements without consolidated financial statements or financial statements in which investments in associates or joint ventures are equity accounted IAS 27 does not directly address the issuance requirements for separate financial statements. In some jurisdictions, an entity that prepares consolidated financial statements is prohibited from issuing its separate financial statements without also publishing its consolidated financial statements. How we see it In our view, an entity that prepares consolidated financial statements can issue its separate financial statements compliant with IAS 27 without also issuing its consolidated financial statements, provided that certain criteria are met as discussed below. The same conditions should be applied by an entity having no subsidiaries that prepares financial statements in which investments in associates or joint ventures are equity accounted for but issues its separate financial statements (in which the investments in associates and joint ventures are not equity accounted) without also issuing its financial statements in which investments in associates or joint ventures are equity accounted. Separate financial statements of a parent entity can also be considered compliant with IAS 27 when the exemption to present consolidated financial statements criteria in IFRS 10.4(a) are met. [IFRS 10.4(a)]. An entity would have to meet the following criteria to issue separate financial statements without also issuing consolidated financial statements: (a) The separate financial statements identify the consolidated financial statements prepared under IFRS 10 to which they relate. [IAS 27.17]. In other words, they must draw attention to the fact that the entity also prepares consolidated financial statements and disclose the address where the consolidated financial statements are available, for example, by providing contact details of a person or an e-mail address from which a hard copy of the document can be obtained or a website address where the consolidated financial statements can be found and downloaded. And (b) The consolidated financial statements have been prepared and approved no later than the date on which the separate financial statements have been approved. Thus, it is not possible to issue the separate financial statements before the consolidated financial statements have been finalised. IFRIC Update, March 2015, p.11. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements IAS 27 requires a parent to identify the consolidated financial statements prepared by the parent where the parent is not exempt from preparing consolidated financial statements. [IAS 27.17]. Therefore, if the parent, or ultimate or intermediate parent, has not issued consolidated financial statements prepared in accordance with IFRS accounting standards at the date the separate financial statements are issued, this requirement cannot be met and therefore the separate financial statements cannot be considered to be in compliance with IAS 27. This will also be the case if the consolidated financial statements are prepared, but are not in accordance with IFRS accounting standards (e.g., prepared in accordance with local GAAP). Frequently asked questions Question 1-3: Can separate financial statements be issued before the respective consolidated financial statements? The matter of whether separate financial statements can be issued before the respective consolidated financial statements was explicitly considered by the IFRS IC in March 2006. The IFRS IC concluded that separate financial statements issued before consolidated financial statements could not comply with IFRS accounting standards as issued by the IASB, because “separate financial statements should identify the financial statements prepared in accordance with paragraph 9 of IAS 27 to which they relate (the consolidated financial statements), unless one of the exemptions provided by paragraph 10 is applicable”.4 [IAS 27.9, IAS 27.10]. Although IAS 27 has changed since the IFRS IC considered that issue, the current version of IAS 27 still requires separate financial statements to identify financial statements prepared in accordance with IFRS 10, IFRS 11 or IAS 28. [IAS 27.17]. It therefore implies that consolidated financial statements should be available before or at the same date as separate financial statements. However, the situation may be different if local requirements set specific rules relating to the timing of publication of financial statements. This is for example, the case for an entity that is incorporated in the European Union (EU), as described in section 1.2 below. Whilst the conclusion reached by the IFRS IC is applicable to a parent that prepares consolidated financial statements, there are mixed views about whether it is applicable by analogy to an entity that is exempted from consolidation in accordance with IFRS 10.4(a), or from applying the equity method in accordance with IAS 28.17 and hence, whether these entities may present separate financial statements as their only financial statements (e.g., intermediate holding companies). [IFRS 10.4(a), IAS 28.17]. Some argue that such intermediate holding companies that use the exemption cannot issue their separate financial statements before the IFRS consolidated financial statements of the parent are issued and available for use as they are required to disclose where such consolidated financial statements are obtainable. [IAS 27.16]. Others believe that the parent’s consolidated financial statements must be periodically produced and be available for public use. Due to the lack of clear guidance, the determination of an acceptable approach may need to consider specific facts and circumstances such as local regulatory requirements and the timing between the issuance of the intermediate parent’s separate financial statements and its parent’s consolidated financial statements. 1.2 Entities incorporated in the EU and consolidated and separate financial statements The EU Regulation on International Accounting Standards requires IFRS accounting standards to be applied by certain entities in their consolidated financial statements. As a result of the EU endorsement mechanism, IFRS accounting standards as adopted by the EU may differ in some respects from the body of Standards and Interpretations issued by the IASB (see Chapter 1 at section 4.2.1). In some circumstances a difference between IFRS accounting standards and IFRS accounting standards as adopted by the European Union may affect separate financial statements. IFRIC Update, March 2006, p.7. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements The IFRS IC concluded that separate financial statements issued before consolidated financial statements cannot comply with IFRS accounting standards as issued by the IASB (see Question 1-3 in section 1.1.3 above). However, in January 2007 the European Commission stated that “the Commission Services are of the opinion that, if a company chooses or is required to prepare its annual accounts in accordance with IFRS accounting standards as adopted by the EU, it can prepare and file them independently from the preparation and filing of its consolidated accounts - and thus in advance, where the national law transposing the Directives requires or permits separate publication”.5 In other words, under ‘IFRS accounting standards as adopted by the EU’ it may be possible to issue separate financial statements before the consolidated financial statements are issued. The details about differences between scope of consolidation under IFRS 10 and European Union national legislation are described in # Chapter 7 at section 2.2.5. Agenda paper for the meeting of the Accounting Regulatory Committee on 2nd February 2007 (document ARC/08/2007), Subject: Relationship between the IAS Regulation and the 4th and 7th Company Law Directives - Can a company preparing both individual and consolidated accounts in accordance with adopted IFRS issue the individual accounts before the consolidated accounts?, European Commission: Internal Market and Services DG: Free movement of capital, company law and corporate governance: Accounting/PB D(2006), 15 January 2007, para. 3.1. Separate and individual financial statements Requirements of separate financial statements In separate financial statements, investments in subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures are accounted for either: • At cost (see section 2.1 below) • In accordance with IFRS 9 (see section 2.2 below) Or • Using the equity method as described in IAS 28 (see section 2.3 below) The above applies to all situations except when such investments are classified as held for sale (or included in a disposal group that is classified as held for sale) in accordance with IFRS 5 Non-current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations. When this is the case, investments in subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures that are accounted for at cost, or using the equity method in the separate financial statements, are measured in accordance with IFRS 5, [IAS 27.10], i.e., at the lower of carrying amount and fair value less costs to sell (see Chapter 5). Investments that are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9 in the separate financial statements and which are classified as held for sale continue to be measured in accordance with IFRS 9 (see Chapter 5 at section 2.2.1). Each ‘category’ of investment must be accounted for consistently. [IAS 27.10]. ‘Category’ is not defined in IFRS accounting standards but is used in a number of Standards. For example, IFRS 7 Financial Instruments: Disclosures uses ‘category’ to refer to groupings of financial assets and financial liabilities that are measured in different ways - for example, financial assets measured at fair value through profit or loss is one category of financial asset and financial assets measured at amortised cost is another category of financial asset. While acknowledging this in the Basis for Conclusions of the Annual Improvements 2014 - 2016 Cycle issued in 2016, the IASB clarified that paragraph 10 of IAS 27 should not be read to mean that, in all circumstances, all investments in associates are one ‘category’ of investment, and that all investments in joint ventures are one ‘category’ of investment. Accordingly, determining multiple categories for each class of investments is not precluded under IFRS accounting standards in all circumstances. Furthermore, while the Annual Improvements 2014-2016 cycle and the related Basis for Conclusions refer to investments in associates and joint ventures, rather than investments in subsidiaries, the same logic could be extended to investments in subsidiaries, given that the accounting treatment of all these investments in the separate financial statements of the parent entity is defined in the same paragraph 10 of IAS 27. When an entity applies the concept of category more granularly, the determination of categories should be based on clear and objective criteria that results in information that is relevant and reliable. An entity could consider, for instance, the guidance in IFRS 7 above or its business model as in the case of investment entities (as discussed below). However, we believe that in identifying categories more granularly an entity shall not consider in isolation only a few criteria such as an investments geography or type of products sold. Judgement is exercised to define criteria to identify categories more granularly. In applying a consistent policy to each category of investment, we believe IAS 27 would consider the categorisation of investments in subsidiaries, separate to associates and joint ventures; therefore, categorisation and the measurement of similar categories does not need to be consistent between classes of investments. How we see it While ‘category’ is not defined, we believe that investments in subsidiaries, associates, and joint ventures can be further divided into more granular categories, only if a category can be defined clearly and objectively and results in information that is relevant and reliable. Judgement is exercised to define criteria to identify categories more granularly and should be consistently applied. When an investment in an associate or joint venture is accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9 in the consolidated financial statements, it must also be accounted for in the same way in the separate financial statements. [IAS 27.11]. The circumstances in which IFRS 9 is applied to account for investments in associates and joint ventures in consolidated financial statements are discussed in Chapter 12 at section 5 and Chapter 13 at section 2.2, respectively. Separate and individual financial statements A parent that meets the definition of an investment entity is required to measure its investments in particular subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 in its consolidated financial statements, [IFRS 10.31], and is required to account for them in the same way in the separate financial statements. [IAS 27.11A]. When an investment entity parent has shares in subsidiaries that only provide investment related services (and therefore are not subject to mandatory fair value measurement), that parent effectively has shares in two categories of subsidiaries. It therefore, has an accounting policy choice to account for those subsidiaries that only provide investment related services at cost, in accordance with IFRS 9, or using the equity method in its separate financial statements. When an entity becomes an investment entity, the difference between the previous carrying value of the investment in a subsidiary and its fair value at the date of change in status of the parent is recognised as a gain or loss in profit or loss. When a parent ceases to be an investment entity, it should follow IAS 27.10 and account for the investment in a subsidiary either at cost (the then fair value of the subsidiary at the date of the change in the status becomes the deemed cost), continue to account for the investment in accordance with IFRS 9, or apply the equity method. [IAS 27.10, IAS 27.11B]. IAS 27 contains specific requirements related to the treatment of dividends from investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures or associates. Such dividends are recognised in profit or loss unless the entity elects to use equity method, in which case the dividend is recognised as a reduction of the carrying amount of the investment. [IAS 27.12]. IAS 36 Impairment of Assets includes specific triggers for impairment reviews on receipt of such dividends. These are discussed in section 2.4.1 below. 2.1 Cost method There is no general definition or description of ‘cost’ in IAS 27. How the term applies in practice is described in section 2.1.1 below. IAS 27 addresses the cost of investment in a new holding company that becomes the parent of an existing parent in a one-for-one share exchange. This is discussed further in sections 2.1.1.E and 2.1.1.F below. IAS 27 also indicates that when an entity ceases to be an investment entity and its accounting policy is to account for investments in subsidiaries, associates or joint ventures at cost, the fair value as at the date of the change in status shall be used as the deemed cost. [IAS 27.11B]. IFRS 1 First-time Adoption of International Financial Reporting Standards allows use of a ‘deemed cost’ as a transitional exemption for those applying IFRS accounting standards for the first time in separate financial statements (see section 2.1.2 below). 2.1.1 Cost of investment IAS 27 does not define what is meant by ‘cost’ except in the specific circumstances of certain types of group reorganisations, described below, and when an entity ceases to be an investment entity and accounts for investments in subsidiaries at cost as indicated above. ‘Consideration given’ is likewise not defined. As discussed further in Chapter 3 at section 4.3, IAS 8 Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and Errors states that, in the absence of specific requirements in IFRS accounting standards, management should first refer to the requirements and guidance in IFRS accounting standards that deal with similar and related issues. The glossary to IFRS accounting standards defines cost as “the amount of cash or cash equivalents paid or the fair value of the other consideration given to acquire an asset at the time of its acquisition or construction”. How we see it When considering what is meant by ‘cost’ in IAS 27 we believe that in applying IAS 8 the key sources of guidance in IFRS accounting standards are: • ‘Consideration transferred’ in the context of a business combination, as referred to in paragraph 37 of IFRS 3 Business Combinations • ‘Cost’ as applied in relation to acquisitions of property, plant and equipment in accordance with IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment, intangible assets in accordance with IAS 38 Intangible Assets and investment property in accordance with IAS 40 Investment Property And • ‘Cost’ in the context of determination of the cost of an investment in an associate as discussed in the agenda decision issued by the IFRS IC in July 2009 Separate and individual financial statements Applying the requirements of IFRS 3, the ‘consideration transferred’ in a business combination comprises the sum of the acquisition-date fair values of assets transferred by the acquirer, liabilities incurred by the acquirer to the former owners of the acquiree, and equity interests issued by the acquirer. [IFRS 3.37]. This includes any liability (or asset) for contingent consideration, which is measured and recognised at fair value at the acquisition date. Subsequent changes in the measurement of the changes in the liability (or asset) are recognised in profit or loss (see Chapter 10 at section 7.1). ‘Cost’ as applied in relation to acquisitions of property, plant and equipment is the amount of cash or cash equivalents paid, or the fair value of the other consideration given, to acquire an asset at the time of its acquisition or construction or, where applicable, the amount attributed to that asset when initially recognised in accordance with the specific requirements of other IFRS accounting standards, e.g., IFRS 2 Share-based Payment. [IAS 16.6]. The IFRS IC and IASB have discussed the topic Variable payments for the separate acquisition of PPE and intangible assets for a few years, attempting to clarify how the initial recognition of the variable payments, such as contingent consideration, and subsequent changes in the value of those payments should be recognised. The scope of the past deliberations did not specifically include the cost of an investment in a subsidiary, associate, or joint venture. However, as they considered general principles about the recognition of variable payments, we believe they can also be considered relevant in determining the cost of such investments. There was diversity of views about whether the liability for contingent consideration relating to the separate acquisition of property, plant and equipment and intangible assets falls within the scope of IAS 37 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets or within the scope of IAS 39 Financial Instruments: Recognition and Measurement (this is still relevant after IFRS 9 adoption as well). This diversity of views affects the initial recognition and subsequent accounting for changes in the value of the contingent consideration. The issue was discussed by the IFRS IC in the past however it was considered too broad to be addressed and was referred to the IASB.6 A critical step in analysing the accounting for variable or contingent consideration in a transaction to acquire an investment in the scope of IAS 27 is to fully understand the contractual arrangements, understanding the nature of the contingent or variable payments. This includes developing a full understanding of whether those contingent payments are linked to the initial value of the asset, or to its future performance, whether the contingency or variability is within or outside of the purchaser’s control and whether the variable payment is a payment for some other goods or services from the seller. In some cases, the contract may establish an obligation for the contingent payment at the outset, whilst in others it might be determined that the arrangement is an executory contract, and the liability to make the contingent payments is only recognised later once either party fulfils its obligations under the contract. The two key issues with regards to the accounting for variable consideration in an asset purchase are 1) whether and when to recognise a liability for that variable consideration, and 2) whether subsequent changes to the estimate of variable or contingent consideration should be accounted for as part of the cost of the acquired asset (see Chapter 19 at section 4.1.9). Looking at the first question of when a liability to make variable or contingent payments would be recognised, if the arrangement is not considered to be an executory contract, one consideration is whether those payments are dependent on the entity’s future activity, or not. IAS 32.25 stipulates that where the contingency is outside the control of both the issuer and the holder, a contingent obligation to deliver cash meets the definition of a financial liability (see Chapter 43 at section 4.3). Therefore, if the obligation to make the variable payment does not depend on the acquiring entity’s future activity, it would seem appropriate to recognise a financial liability in respect of the contingent amounts. [IAS 32.25]. However, it is acknowledged that whilst this view was tentatively agreed during the course of the IFRS IC discussions on the topic, ultimately, this was not clarified or confirmed as part of the final agenda decision. Furthermore, the final agenda decision notes that there are questions about the accounting for variable payments subsequent to the purchase of the asset, and in making that statement the IFRS IC did not distinguish between the different types of variable payments. IFRIC Update, March 2016, p.5. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements How we see it In light of the discussions held by the IFRS IC and the absence of clear guidance in the final agenda decision, entities will need to develop and apply an accounting policy in respect of whether and when the obligation to make contingent payments is recognised as a liability. In doing so, entities will need to apply the hierarchy in IAS 8. The policy selected should result in information that is relevant and reliable in the particular circumstances and must be consistently applied to similar transactions. Different payments may have different types of variability. Therefore, it might be appropriate for an entity to have different policies for dissimilar transactions. If information about the selected accounting policy is expected to be material to users of the financial statements, that accounting policy information should be disclosed. As described above, there is no explicit guidance on the treatment of variable or contingent consideration in the acquisition of an asset and there is diversity in practice for these types of arrangements. Therefore, examples of some accounting policies an entity may want to consider regarding initial recognition of the asset include the following: • The cost of the asset on initial recognition includes the fair value of all variable or contingent consideration at the date of acquisition. A corresponding liability is recognised for the same amount. • The cost of the asset on initial recognition excludes any variable or contingent payments. Accordingly, no liability is recognised for those contingent or variable amounts. The second question that needs to be addressed is how the entity accounts for subsequent payments and changes to the estimate of variable or contingent consideration. Depending on the facts and circumstances of the arrangement an entity may determine that: 1) Variability arises from exchange rates, time value of money, or embedded derivatives - Changes in the carrying amount of a recognised liability that arise from exchange rates are accounted for in accordance with IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates and IFRIC 22 Foreign Currency Transactions and Advance Consideration, while the time value of money and embedded derivatives are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9; 2) Variability is directly linked to the quality or quantity of the asset(s) received - In these cases the initial recognition of the asset and liability were based on an accounting estimate that is subsequently updated in accordance with IAS 8.37; or 3) The variability in the arrangement does not fall under (1) or (2) above and an accounting policy needs to be developed to address it. In the latter case, an entity will need to apply the requirements of IAS 8 to develop an accounting policy that it should apply consistently to all similar transactions. The accounting policy developed will need to address whether the change in the estimate of variable or contingent consideration should be capitalised as part of the cost of the asset or expensed. This determination is usually based on the reason for the contingent payment and entities should consider the substance of the arrangement in determining the appropriate treatment. How we see it Until this matter is resolved by the IASB, where the accounting for the variability or contingency is not prescribed in another standard, an entity should adopt and apply a consistent accounting policy in relation to whether subsequent payments or changes to the measurement of a recognised liability to make contingent or variable payments are included as part of the cost of the investment or expensed as incurred, in accordance with the hierarchy in IAS 8. An entity should exercise judgement in developing and consistently applying an accounting policy that results in information that is relevant and reliable in the particular circumstances. If information about the selected accounting policy is expected to be material to users of the financial statements, that accounting policy information should be disclosed. Similar challenges to the above arise when the seller in a business combination contractually agrees to indemnify the acquirer for the outcome of a contingency or uncertainty related to all or part of a specific asset or liability (see Chapter 10 at section 5.6.4). Such indemnification agreements often relate to uncertain tax positions or litigations. Whilst there is guidance about how to deal with the resulting indemnification assets in the consolidated financial statements of the acquirer, there is no comparable guidance about the accounting of such indemnification agreements in the separate financial statements of the acquirer. Separate and individual financial statements How we see it We believe that as these indemnification assets have some similarities to contingent consideration, an entity can apply the guidance explained in the box above by analogy in developing its accounting policy for indemnification agreements. Contingent consideration is discussed in relation to intangible assets, property, plant and equipment and investment property in Chapter 18 at section 4.5, Chapter 19 at section 4.1.9 and Chapter 20 at section 4.10 respectively. Frequently asked questions Question 2-1: How are transaction costs relating to the acquisition of an investment in a subsidiary, associate or joint venture treated in the separate financial statements? In July 2009, the IFRS IC discussed the determination of the initial carrying amount of an equity method investment, which under IAS 28 is at cost, and noted that IFRS accounting standards consistently require assets not measured at fair value through profit or loss to be measured on initial recognition at cost. Generally stated, ‘cost’ includes the purchase price and other costs directly attributable to the acquisition or issuance of the asset such as professional fees for legal services, transfer taxes and other transaction costs.7 How we see it Given that IAS 27 does not separately define ‘cost’, we believe it is appropriate to apply a general meaning of ‘cost’ in determining the cost of investments in subsidiaries, associates or joint ventures in separate financial statements based on the IFRS IC discussion in July 2009. Consequently, we believe that the cost of investment in a subsidiary in the separate financial statements includes any transaction costs incurred even though such costs are expensed in the consolidated financial statements. 2.1.1.A Investments acquired for own shares or other equity instruments This section relates to the acquisition of an investment in exchange for own shares and other equity instruments. Guidance on the acquisition of an investment in exchange for an owned investment is dealt in section 4.4.1.B below. Transfers of assets in group restructuring arrangements may fall in the scope of IFRS 2 (see Chapter 30 at section 2.2.3.B); further guidance is also included in section 4.4.1 below in this Chapter. In some jurisdictions, local law may permit investments acquired for an issue of shares to be recorded at a notional value (for example, the nominal value of the shares issued). In our view, this is not an appropriate measure of ‘cost’ under IFRS accounting standards. How we see it In our view, an investment in a subsidiary, associate or joint venture acquired in exchange for an issue of shares or other equity instruments is recorded at the fair value of the consideration given (being the fair value of equity instruments issued) or the assets received, if that could be more reliably measured, together with directly attributable transaction costs. A transaction in which an investment in a subsidiary, associate or joint venture is acquired in exchange for an issue of shares or other equity instruments is not specifically addressed under IFRS accounting standards, since it falls outside the scope of both IFRS 9 and IFRS 2 (see Chapter 30 at section 2.2.3.F). 2.1.1.B Investments acquired in common control transactions An investment in a subsidiary, associate or joint venture could also be acquired in a common control transaction. Section 4.4.1 below discusses transactions between entities under common control involving non-monetary assets, while section 4.4 below discusses the concept of substance that can be relevant when analysing similar transactions. IFRIC Update, July 2009, p.3. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements How we see it When an investment in a subsidiary, associate or joint venture is acquired in a common control transaction, in our view, the cost is generally measured at the fair value of the consideration given (be it cash, other assets or additional shares) plus, where applicable, any costs directly attributable to the acquisition. However, when the purchase consideration does not correspond to the fair value of the investment acquired, in our view, the acquirer has an accounting policy choice to account for the investment at the fair value of consideration given or can impute an equity contribution or dividend distribution and in effect account for the investment at its fair value. Illustration 2-1 below illustrates the determination of the cost of an investment in a subsidiary in separate financial statements as described above. Illustration 2-1: Cost of a subsidiary in separate financial statements when the pooling of interest method is applied in consolidated financial statements Parent has a 100% direct interest in Sub 1 and Sub 2. As part of a group reorganisation, the parent transfers its direct interest in Sub 2 to Sub 1 in exchange for consideration of: Scenario 1 - CU200 (equal to the fair value of Sub 2). Scenario 2 - CU150. The carrying amount of the investment in Sub 2 in the separate financial statements of Parent is CU50. The carrying amount of Sub 2’s net assets in the separate financial statements of Sub 2 is CU110. Sub 1 accounts for the acquisition of Sub 2 using the pooling of interest method in its consolidated financial statements. In Scenario 1, the cost of an investment in a subsidiary that is acquired as part of a group reorganisation is the fair value of the consideration given (be it cash, other assets, or additional shares), plus, where applicable, any costs directly attributable to the acquisition. Therefore, the cost to Sub 1 is CU200. The cost is not the carrying amount of the investment in Sub 2 in the separate financial statements of Parent (i.e., CU50), nor the carrying amount of Sub 2’s net assets in the separate financial statements of Sub 2 (i.e., CU110). In Scenario 2, the conclusion in Scenario 1 applies even if the fair value of the consideration given is less than the fair value of the acquiree. Therefore, the cost of investment is CU150. However, the acquirer may choose to recognise an equity element (equity contribution). In this case, the cost of investment is CU200 with CU50 recognised as an equity contribution. Frequently asked questions Question 2-2: How does a new intermediate parent established within a group determine the cost of its investments in subsidiaries when it accounts for those investments at cost in accordance with IAS 27.10(a)? In July and September 2011, the IFRS IC discussed group reorganisations in separate financial statements in response to a request asking for clarification on how entities that are established as new intermediate parents within a group determine the cost of their investments in subsidiaries when they account for those investments at cost in accordance with IAS 27.10(a). In the agenda decision issued, the IFRS IC noted that the normal basis for determining the cost of an investment in a subsidiary under IAS 27.10(a) has to be applied to reorganisations that result in the new intermediate parent having more than one direct subsidiary.8 [IAS 27.10(a)]. Some have read this to mean that ‘the normal basis for determining the cost of investment’ in a common control transaction is not restricted to using the fair value of the consideration given, but that another basis for determining cost may be appropriate. How we see it One situation when we believe that it would be acceptable not to use the fair value of the consideration given is for a common control transaction in which an investment in a subsidiary constituting a business is acquired in a share-for-share exchange. In that circumstance, we believe that it is also acceptable to measure the cost based on the carrying amount of the investment in the subsidiary in the transferor entity’s separate financial statements immediately prior to the transaction, rather than at the fair value of the shares given as consideration. This approach is conceptually based on the pooling of interest approach that is allowed for business combinations under common control (see Chapter 11 at section 3). IFRIC Update, September 2011, p.3. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements Common control transactions are discussed further in section 4 below. There are specific measurement requirements applicable to certain arrangements involving the formation of a new parent or intermediate parent, which are described in sections 2.1.1.E and 2.1.1.F below. 2.1.1.C Cost of investment in subsidiary, associate or joint venture acquired in stages It may be that an investment in a subsidiary, associate or joint venture is acquired in stages and that, up to the date on which control, significant influence or joint control is first achieved, the initial investment was accounted for at fair value under IFRS 9. This raises the question of what the carrying amount should be in the separate financial statements when the cost method is applied. Frequently asked questions Question 2-3: How does an entity account for an acquired additional interest in the investee (that is not an associate, joint venture, or subsidiary of the entity) which results in the entity obtaining control of the investee i.e., the investee becomes a subsidiary of the entity? In January 2019, the IFRS IC discussed the following fact pattern: the entity holds an initial investment in another entity (investee) that is an investment in an equity instrument as defined in paragraph 11 of IAS 32 Financial Instruments: Presentation; the investee is not an associate, joint venture or subsidiary of the entity and, accordingly, the entity applies IFRS 9 in accounting for its initial investment (initial interest). Subsequently the entity acquires an additional interest in the investee (additional interest), which results in the entity obtaining control of the investee, i.e., the investee becomes a subsidiary of the entity. The IFRS IC observed that IAS 27 does not define ‘cost’, nor does it specify how an entity determines the cost of an investment acquired in stages and concluded that a reasonable reading of the requirements in IFRS accounting standards could result in the application of either one of the following two approaches: • Approach 1 Fair value as deemed cost approach Under this approach, the transaction is viewed as if the entity is exchanging its initial interest (plus consideration paid for the additional interest) for a controlling interest in the investee (exchange view). As a result, the cost of investment will be determined as the fair value of the initial interest at the date of obtaining control of the subsidiary, plus any consideration paid for the additional interest. • Approach 2 Accumulated cost approach Under this approach, the transaction is viewed as if the entity is purchasing the additional interest while retaining the initial interest (non-exchange view). As a result, the cost of investment will be determined as the consideration paid for the initial interest (original consideration), plus any consideration paid for the additional interest. In the exchange view the step acquisition transaction is considered a significant economic event - this is because an entity obtains control. The parallel can be drawn, for example, to a business combination achieved in stages, [IFRS 3.BC384], or an entity that ceases to be an investment entity. [IFRS 10.BC271]. In these situations, the fair value of the existing investment is deemed to be the consideration paid at the date of the transaction or event. Alternatively, the accumulated cost approach considers both the initial acquisition of an interest that is neither an interest in a subsidiary, an associate or a joint venture, and the acquisition of the controlling stake to be separate transactions. The application of either one of the two approaches is an accounting policy choice and hence it should be applied consistently to all step acquisition transactions. The selected approach is disclosed in accordance with requirements of paragraphs 117-124 of IAS 1 Presentation of Financial Statements. [IAS 1.117-124]. Although both approaches are acceptable, the IFRS IC decided to report to the Board that, in their view, the fair value as deemed cost approach would provide more useful information to users of financial statements than the accumulated cost approach.9 The IFRS IC also considered how an entity should account for any difference between the fair value of the initial interest at the date of obtaining control and its original consideration when applying the accumulated cost approach. The IFRS IC concluded that the difference meets the definitions of income or expense in the Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting. Accordingly, the IFRS IC concluded that, the entity recognises this difference in profit or loss, regardless of whether, before obtaining control, the investment had been measured at fair value through profit and loss or fair value through other comprehensive income, in accordance with IAS 1.88. [IAS 1.88]. IFRIC Update, January 2019, p.8. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements The two approaches are further explained in the following Illustration 2-2: Illustration 2-2: Cost of a subsidiary acquired in stages Entity A has a 10% interest in Entity B, which it acquired in January 20X2 for CU300. This investment is a financial asset measured at fair value in accordance with IFRS 9, in both the consolidated and separate financial statements of Entity A for the six months ended 30 June 20X2. On 1 July 20X2, Entity A acquires a further 45% interest in Entity B for CU2,160 (its then fair value), giving Entity A control over Entity B. The original 10% interest has a fair value of CU480 at that date. In addition, transaction costs were incurred amounting to CU50. Approach 1 - Fair value as deemed cost approach The cost of investment after both transactions is the sum of the fair value of the initial interest at the date of obtaining control of the subsidiary plus any consideration paid for the additional interest plus transaction costs - CU2,690 (CU480 + CU2,160 + CU50). Approach 2 - Accumulated cost approach The cost of investment after both transactions is the sum of the consideration given for each tranche plus transaction costs - CU2,510 (CU300 + CU2,160 + CU50). The reversal of increase in the fair value of CU180 (CU480 - CU300) relating to the initial 10% interest is recognised in profit or loss, regardless of whether the entity accounted for the initial interest at fair value through other comprehensive income or at fair value through profit or loss. If changes to the carrying amount of the investment had resulted from an impairment charge, this charge may not necessarily be reversed. This is because the investment must still be considered for impairment in the separate financial statements of the investor. How we see it Although the January 2019 agenda decision discussed above does not deal with IAS 28, we believe that based on this agenda decision, the fair value as deemed cost approach and the accumulated cost approach could also be applied by analogy to step acquisition transactions in which an existing investment accounted for under IFRS 9 subsequently becomes an associate or a joint venture, to which the equity method is applied. 2.1.1.D Investment in a subsidiary accounted for at cost: partial disposal An entity that accounts for its investments in subsidiaries at cost in accordance with IAS 27.10 may dispose of parts of its investment in a subsidiary so that following the disposal the entity either maintains control or has joint control or significant influence over the investee. This raises the question of how the carrying amount of the retained interest should be determined in the separate financial statements. How we see it When the cost method is applied to all investments in subsidiaries, associates, and joint ventures we believe that no remeasurement of cost should occur on reclassification between types of investments. As a result, the change in the carrying amount of the investment should be proportionate to the part disposed of. IAS 27 does not define cost (see section 2.1 above) and consequently there is no specific guidance on how to calculate the cost of investments in subsidiaries for derecognition purposes. IFRS 9 also contains no specific guidance for the purpose of derecognition of financial assets. Under IAS 39, which was also silent on this topic, entities chose between three cost allocation methods for available-for-sale securities: the average cost method, the first-in-first-out (FIFO) method, or the specific identification method. Specific identification can be applied if the entity is able to identify the specific items sold and their costs. For example, a specific security may be identified as sold by linking the date, amount and cost of securities bought with the sale transaction, provided that there is no other evidence suggesting that the actual security sold was not the one identified under this method. How we see it We believe that, in the absence of specific guidance, entities can elect to apply a FIFO approach, a specific identification method, or the average cost method constituting acceptable accounting policy choices to be applied consistently (see Chapter 47 at section 6.6). Separate and individual financial statements If, following the disposal, the entity has neither control, joint control nor significant influence over the investee, a question arises as to how the carrying amount of the retained interest should be determined in the separate financial statements. Frequently asked questions Question 2-4: How is a transaction accounted for when an entity disposes of part of its investment in a subsidiary, that was previously accounted for at cost in accordance with IAS 27.10, and following the disposal the entity has neither control, joint control nor significant influence over the investee? In January 2019, the IFRS IC concluded that IAS 27.9 requires an entity to apply all applicable IFRS accounting standard in preparing its separate financial statements, except when accounting for investments in subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures to which IAS 27.10 applies.10 [IAS 27.9, IAS 27.10]. After the partial disposal transaction, the investee is not a subsidiary, associate, or joint venture of the entity. Accordingly, the entity applies IFRS 9 for the first time in accounting for its retained interest in the investee. The IFRS IC observed that the presentation election in IFRS 9.4.1.4 applies at initial recognition of an investment in an equity instrument, which permits the holder of particular investments in equity instruments to present subsequent changes in fair value in other comprehensive income. [IFRS 9.4.1.4]. Therefore, an investment in an equity instrument within the scope of IFRS 9 is eligible for the election if it is neither held for trading (as defined in Appendix A of IFRS 9) nor contingent consideration recognised by an acquirer in a business combination to which IFRS 3 applies. Assuming the retained interest is not held for trading in the fact pattern discussed by the IFRS IC: (a) The retained interest is eligible for the presentation election in IFRS 9.4.1.4 And (b) The entity would make this presentation election when it first applies IFRS 9 to the retained interest (i.e., at the date of losing control of the investee)11 Any difference between the cost of the retained interest and its fair value on the date the entity loses control of the investee meets the definitions of income or expense in the Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting. Accordingly, the IFRS IC concluded that, applying IAS 1.88, the entity recognises this difference in profit or loss. [IAS 1.88]. This is the case regardless of whether the entity presents subsequent changes in fair value of the retained interest in profit or loss or other comprehensive income. The IFRS IC also noted that its conclusion is consistent with the requirements in IAS 28.22(b) and IAS 27.11B, which deal with similar and related issues. [IAS 28.22(b), IAS 27.11B]. The application of the IFRS IC’s conclusion is explained in the following Illustration 2-3: Illustration 2-3: Partial disposal of a subsidiary accounted for at cost when an investor retains an interest that is neither a subsidiary, associate nor joint venture Entity A has a 60% interest in Entity B, which it acquired for CU2,700 in 20X0. This investment is measured at cost of CU2,700 in the separate financial statements of Entity A at 30 June 20X3. On 1 July 20X3, Entity A sells a 50% interest in Entity B for CU3,000 (its then fair value) and retains a 10% interest in Entity B. The fair value of the 10% interest is CU600 at that date. As the retained interest is not held for trading, the entity elects to account for the retained interest at fair value through other comprehensive income. The total gain from the partial disposal to be recognised in profit or loss amounts to CU900 (CU3,000 + CU600 - CU2,700). IFRIC Update, January 2019, p.4. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, January 2019, p.4. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements 2.1.1.E Formation of a new parent IAS 27 has the following specific guidance when a new parent is formed above an existing parent: [IAS 27.13] Extract from IAS 27 When a parent reorganises the structure of its group by establishing a new entity as its parent in a manner that satisfies the following criteria: (a) the new parent obtains control of the original parent by issuing equity instruments in exchange for existing equity instruments of the original parent; (b) the assets and liabilities of the new group and the original group are the same immediately before and after the reorganisation; and (c) the owners of the original parent before the reorganisation have the same absolute and relative interests in the net assets of the original group and the new group immediately before and after the reorganisation, and the new parent accounts for its investment in the original parent in accordance with paragraph 10(a) in its separate financial statements, the new parent shall measure cost at the carrying amount of its share of the equity items shown in the separate financial statements of the original parent at the date of the reorganisation. The same guidance is also applicable if the entity that puts a new parent between it and the shareholders is not itself a parent, i.e., it has no subsidiaries. In such cases, references in the three conditions to ‘original parent’ and ‘original group’ are to the ‘original entity’. [IAS 27.14]. The types of reorganisation to which these requirements apply involves an existing entity and its shareholders agreeing to create a new parent between them without changing either the composition of the group or their own absolute and relative interests. This is not a general rule that applies to all common control transactions. Transfers of subsidiaries from the ownership of one entity to another within a group are not within the scope. The IASB deliberately excluded extending the amendment to other types of reorganisations or to common control transactions more generally because of its plans, now superseded as described below, to address this in its project on common control transactions. [IAS 27.BC27]. The formation of a new parent - calculating the cost and measuring equity is further discussed in section 2.1.1.F below. The IASB in December 2012 identified business combinations under common control as a priority research project. In June 2014, the Board tentatively decided that the research project should consider business combinations under common control and group restructurings.12 The Discussion Paper Business Combinations under Common Control was issued for public comment in November 2020.13 The Discussion Paper does not address how a receiving company should report in its separate financial statements an investment in a subsidiary received in a business combination under common control as this topic is addressed by IAS 27.14 However, if the combination involves the transfer of an unincorporated business, the possible reporting requirements developed in this project would also apply in separate financial statements prepared by the receiving company. In April 2024, the IASB published a project summary that explained the reasons behind its decision in November 2023 not to develop requirements for reporting business combinations under common control; namely, the IASB acknowledged that information needed by investors varied among jurisdictions, making it difficult to develop requirements that would meet the information needs of users globally. Furthermore, the IASB acknowledged that diversity in reporting on business combinations under common control would continue and observed that investors said that they can work with this diversity. This implies that entities - in the absence of authoritative guidance issued by the IASB - will continue to account for such common control transactions in accordance with their accounting policies (see section 4.4 below and Chapter 11 at section 3). *Reference: IASB Update, June 2014, p.9. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Discussion Paper DP/2020/2: Business Combinations under Common Control, IASB, November 2020. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Discussion Paper DP/2020/2: Business Combinations under Common Control, IASB, November 2020, 1.23, p.19. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Separate and individual financial statements As well as the establishment of a new ultimate parent of a group, arrangements that could meet the criteria in IAS 27.13, [IAS 27.13], mentioned above include the following: (a) Reorganisations in which the new parent does not acquire all classes of the equity instruments issued by the original parent. For example, the original parent may have preference shares that are classified as equity in addition to ordinary shares; the new parent does not have to acquire the preference shares for the transaction to be within scope. [IAS 27.BC24(a)]. (b) A new parent obtains control of the original parent without acquiring all the ordinary shares of the original parent. [IAS 27.BC24(a)]. However, the absolute and relative holdings must be the same immediately before and after the transaction. [IAS 27.13(c)]. The requirements will apply, for example, if a controlling group of shareholders insert a new entity between themselves and the original parent that holds all their original shares, and the new parent is formed in a manner that does not change the relative ownership interests of the owners of the group and does not change the equity, assets, and liabilities of the group as before. This is further explained in the following Illustration 2-4: Illustration 2-4: Formation of new parent that does not acquire all the original parent’s ordinary shares Shareholders A and B each hold 50% of the equity instruments of Original Parent. A and B transfer their shares to New Parent in a share-for-share exchange so that both now hold 50% of the shares in New Parent. The absolute and relative interests of A and B and those of the other shareholders in Original Parent are unchanged, so the arrangement is a reorganisation to which the cost method for reorganisations applies. (c) The establishment of an intermediate parent within a group. [IAS 27.BC24(b)]. The principle is exactly the same as inserting a new parent company over the top of a group. ‘Original Parent’ will be an intermediate company within a group, owned by another group company. If the transaction is within scope, the intermediate parent will acquire Original Parent from its parent (the Owner) in a share for share swap. The group structure before and after the transaction can be summarised as follows: If the composition of the underlying group changes, perhaps because the intermediate parent acquires only part of that group or because it acquires another subsidiary as part of the reorganisation, then the arrangement will not be within scope as the assets and liabilities of the group immediately after the reorganisation would not be ‘the same’ as those immediately before the reorganisation. [IAS 27.13(b)]. However, there may be arrangements when, to establish an intermediate parent, shares must be issued for an amount of cash, the value of which is typically driven by various legal aspects.
